 Abstract. 
    
 Using the composition method, we establish the  e e -positivity of spiders of the form  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1) , as conjectured by Aliniaeifard, van Willigenburg and Wang. Following a divide-and-conquer strategy, we group one or two positive-coefficient  e J e_{J} -terms with each negative-coefficient  e I e_{I} -term, where each  J J  is a composition obtained by rearranging the parts of the composition  I I , and prove that the resulting total coefficient is nonnegative. Our main contribution is the explicit construction of the required injections. 
  
 
 
 Key words and phrases:  chromatic symmetric function;  e e -positivity; Stanley and Stembridge’s conjecture; the composition method
 
 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification 05E05, 05C15, 05A20
 
 
 1.  Introduction 

 
 In his seminal paper,  Stanley [6]  introduced the chromatic symmetric function  X G X_{G}  of a graph  G G  as a symmetric-function analogue of Birkhoff’s chromatic polynomial. From the beginning, a basic problem has been to understand the expansion of  X G X_{G}  in the standard bases of the algebra of symmetric functions. Given a basis  { b λ } \{b_{\lambda}\}  for symmetric functions, a symmetric function  f f  is said to be  b b -positive if every  b b -coefficient of  f f  is nonnegative. A graph is said to be  b b -positive if its chromatic symmetric function is  b b -positive.  Stanley [6, Section 5]  asked the question: Which graphs are  e e -positive? 
 
 
 The  e e -basis is especially central because of the conjecture of  Stanley and Stembridge [8] . In the language of chromatic symmetric functions, Stanley and Stembridge’s conjecture asserts that the incomparability graph of any  ( 3 + 1 ) (3+1) -free poset is  e e -positive. Although this is a rather special class of graphs, the conjecture remained open for more than three decades, and was recently confirmed by  Hikita [4, Corollary 1.8] . This illustrates the intrinsic difficulty of proving  e e -positivity. 
 
 
 A complete classification of  e e -positive graphs appears to be out of reach at present. Consequently, much of the current work focuses on identifying families of graphs whose chromatic symmetric functions are  e e -positive. For example,  Tom and Vailaya [10]  established the  e e -positivity of the sum graph of natural unit interval graphs and cycles, and that of noncrossing cycle-chord graphs. Very recently,  Chen et al. [1]  showed the  e e -positivity of clocks, which do not belong to any of the graph classes mentioned above. The general question of which graphs are  e e -positive is still open. 
 
 
 Trees form a particularly important testing ground for  e e -positivity. On the one hand, they are among the structurally simplest connected graphs. On the other hand,  e e -positivity appears to be rare among trees, and even finding an infinite family of  e e -positive trees is difficult. This scarcity is already visible from direct computation: among the  11,989,764 11{,}989{,}764  non-isomorphic connected graphs of order at most  10 10 ,  3,396,886 3{,}396{,}886  are  e e -positive; whereas among the  522,959 522{,}959  non-isomorphic trees of order at most  19 19 , only  51 51  are  e e -positive. In these ranges, the corresponding proportions of  e e -positive graphs are 
 

 
 
 3,396,886 11,989,764 ≈ 28.3316 % and 51 522,959 ≈ 0.009752 % , \frac{3{,}396{,}886}{11{,}989{,}764}\approx 28.3316\%\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{51}{522{,}959}\approx 0.009752\%, 
 
 
 respectively. These comparisons help explain the interest in explicit infinite families of  e e -positive trees, including the family considered in this paper. 
 
 
 A parallel direction is the study of non- e e -positivity of trees.  Dahlberg et al. [3, Conjecture 6.1]  conjectured that every  e e -positive tree  T T  has maximum degree  Δ ⁡ ( T ) \Delta(T)  at most  3 3 .  Tom [9, Theorems 1.5 and 1.6]  made significant progress by proving non- e e -positivity for the following cases: 
 
 •  
 
 trees with  Δ ⁡ ( T ) ≥ 5 \Delta(T)\geq 5 ; 
 
 •  
 
 trees with  Δ ⁡ ( T ) = 4 \Delta(T)=4  and at least one degree- 4 4  vertex that is not adjacent to any leaf; 
 
 •  
 
 spiders with exactly four legs. 
 
 
 Thus, except for the settled case of  4 4 -legged spiders, the remaining maximum-degree- 4 4  trees are those in which every degree- 4 4  vertex is adjacent to a leaf. 
 
 
 Indeed, spiders play an essential role in this direction. For any partition  λ = λ 1 ​ ⋯ ​ λ d \lambda=\lambda_{1}\dotsm\lambda_{d}  of  n − 1 n-1  with  d ≥ 3 d\geq 3 , the  spider   S ⁡ ( λ ) S(\lambda)  is the  n n -vertex tree consisting of paths of lengths  λ 1 , … , λ d \lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d}  with a common end. For instance, the spider  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1)  is obtained by adding a pendant vertex to the path of order  6 ​ m + 3 6m+3 , see  Fig.   1.1 . The importance of spiders is explained by connected partition obstructions.  Dahlberg et al. [3, Lemma 13]  showed that, after deleting a vertex of degree at least  3 3  from a connected graph  G G  and recording the sizes of the resulting connected components as a partition  λ \lambda , connected partition obstructions for the spider  S ⁡ ( λ ) S(\lambda)  give corresponding obstructions for the  e e -positivity of  G G . Together with the criterion of  Wolfgang III [13, Proposition 1.3.3] , which states that any connected  e e -positive graph has a connected partition of every type, this makes spiders a basic testing class for  e e -positivity questions on trees. 
 
 length  4 ​ m + 2 4m+2 length  2 ​ m 2m 
 Figure 1.1.  The spider  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1) . 
 
 
 Wang and Wang [11, Conjecture 6.1]  conjectured that a  3 3 -legged spider  S ⁡ ( a , b , c ) S(a,\,b,\,c)  with  a ≥ b ≥ c ≥ 3 a\geq b\geq c\geq 3  is  e e -positive if and only if it is  S ⁡ ( 8 , 5 , 3 ) S(8,5,3)  or  S ⁡ ( 14 , 9 , 5 ) S(14,9,5) . In contrast, for spiders  S ⁡ ( a , b , c ) S(a,\,b,\,c)  with  c ∈ { 1 , 2 } c\in\{1,2\} , the existing results suggest a much less uniform picture; there appears to be no clear pattern governing whether a given such spider is  e e -positive. Against this background,  Zheng [14, Conjecture 6.2]  presented  Conjecture   1.1  and attributed it to Aliniaeifard, van Willigenburg and Wang. 
 
 
 Conjecture 1.1   (Aliniaeifard, van Willigenburg and Wang) . 
 
 For any integer  m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 , the spider  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1)  is  e e -positive. 
 
 
 
 For example, the  e e -positivity of  S ⁡ ( 6 ,  2 ,  1 ) S(6,\,2,\,1)  is clear from the  e e -expansion 
 

 
 
 X S ⁡ ( 6 , 2 , 1 ) \displaystyle X_{S(6,2,1)} 
 = 10 ​ e 10 + 17 ​ e 91 + 22 ​ e 82 + 7 ​ e 81 2 + 11 ​ e 73 + 24 ​ e 721 + 38 ​ e 64 + 32 ​ e 631 + 26 ​ e 62 2 + 5 ​ e 621 2 \displaystyle=10e_{10}+17e_{91}+22e_{82}+7e_{81^{2}}+11e_{73}+24e_{721}+38e_{64}+32e_{631}+26e_{62^{2}}+5e_{621^{2}} 
 
 
 
 
 + 20 ​ e 5 2 + 55 ​ e 541 + 37 ​ e 532 + 16 ​ e 531 2 + 20 ​ e 52 2 ​ 1 + 42 ​ e 4 2 ​ 2 + 9 ​ e 4 2 ​ 1 2 + e 43 2 + 59 ​ e 4321 \displaystyle\quad+20e_{5^{2}}+55e_{541}+37e_{532}+16e_{531^{2}}+20e_{52^{2}1}+42e_{4^{2}2}+9e_{4^{2}1^{2}}+e_{43^{2}}+59e_{4321} 
 
 
 
 
 + 22 ​ e 42 3 + 3 ​ e 42 2 ​ 1 2 + 8 ​ e 3 3 ​ 1 + 9 ​ e 3 2 ​ 2 2 + 8 ​ e 3 2 ​ 21 2 + 9 ​ e 32 3 ​ 1 + 2 ​ e 2 5 . \displaystyle\quad+22e_{42^{3}}+3e_{42^{2}1^{2}}+8e_{3^{3}1}+9e_{3^{2}2^{2}}+8e_{3^{2}21^{2}}+9e_{32^{3}1}+2e_{2^{5}}. 
 
 
 
 
 The following more general conjecture can be found in  Zheng [14, Conjecture 6.3] . 
 
 
 Conjecture 1.2   (Aliniaeifard, van Willigenburg and Wang) . 
 
 For any integers  m , n ≥ 1 m,n\geq 1 , the spider  S ⁡ ( n ⁡ ( n ! ​ m + 1 ) , n ! ​ m ,  1 ) S(n(n!m+1),\,n!m,\,1)  is  e e -positive. 
 
 
 
 It is true for  n = 1 n=1 , since 
 

 
 
 X S ⁡ ( m + 1 , m ,  1 ) = 1 2 ​ ( X B ( m + 1 ) ​ ( m + 1 ) + e 1 ​ X P 2 ​ m + 2 ) . X_{S(m+1,\,m,\,1)}=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{\big(}{X_{B_{(m+1)(m+1)}}+e_{1}X_{P_{2m+2}}}\mathclose{\big)}. 
 
 
 Here  B ( m + 1 ) ​ ( m + 1 ) B_{(m+1)(m+1)}  denotes the generalized bull graph obtained by adding
a path of length  m m  to each of two distinct vertices of a triangle. This graph
is  e e -positive; see  Dahlberg et al. [2, Page 2681] . For  n = 2 n=2 ,  Conjecture   1.2  reduces to  Conjecture   1.1 . 
 
 
 This paper confirms  Conjecture   1.1 . Our proof uses the composition method and proceeds by decomposing the chromatic symmetric function of  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1)  into several components. The first components are directly  e e -positive, and the remaining component is handled by a divide-and-conquer argument on its  e I e_{I} -terms. More precisely, we group one or two positive  e J e_{J} -terms with each negative  e I e_{I} -term, where the composition  J J  is obtained by rearranging the parts of  I I , and then prove that the total coefficient is nonnegative. The main point is an explicit construction of the required injection, which shows that the positive terms used in this cancellation procedure are all distinct. 
 
 
 In  Section   2 , we recall the necessary notation and the formulas for chromatic symmetric functions of paths and  3 3 -legged spiders. The proof of  Conjecture   1.1  is given in  Section   3 . 
 
 
 
 2.  Preliminary 

 
 Let  n n  be a nonnegative integer. A  composition  of  n n  is a sequence of positive integers that sum to  n n , denoted 
 

 
 
 I = i 1 ⋯ i l ⊨ n . I=i_{1}\cdots i_{l}\vDash n. 
 
 
 In particular, the unique composition of  0 0  is the empty sequence. We denote 
 

 
 
 \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | I \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = n and ℓ ⁡ ( I ) = l . \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{I}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=n\quad\text{and}\quad\ell(I)=l. 
 
 
 The integers  i k i_{k}  are called  parts  of  I I , and we denote the  j j th last part  i ℓ ⁡ ( I ) + 1 − j i_{\ell(I)+1-j}  by  i − j i_{-j}  for notational convenience. Whenever a capital letter such as  I , J ​ … I,J\dots  denotes a composition, we use the corresponding small letter counterparts  i , j ​ … i,j\dots  with integer subscripts to denote its parts. Following  Wang and Zhou [12, Eq. (2.12)] , the  a a -surplus  of  I I  is the number 
 

 
 
 Θ I ( a ) = min { \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | i 1 ⋯ i k \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | : 0 ≤ k ≤ ℓ ( I ) , \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | i 1 ⋯ i k \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | ≥ a } − a . \Theta_{I}(a)=\min\mathopen{\big\{}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{i_{1}\dotsm i_{k}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}\colon 0\leq k\leq\ell(I),\ \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{i_{1}\dotsm i_{k}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}\geq a}\mathclose{\big\}}-a. 
 
 
 It is clear that  Θ I ​ ( a ) ≥ 0 \Theta_{I}(a)\geq 0 . A  partition  of  n n  is a composition of  n n  in non-increasing order, denoted as 
 

 
 
 λ = λ 1 λ 2 ⋯ ⊢ n , where  λ 1 ≥ λ 2 ≥ ⋯ ≥ 1 . \lambda=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\cdots\vdash n,\quad\text{where $\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 1$}. 
 
 
 
 
 A  symmetric function  over a commutative ring  R R  with identity is a formal power series  f ⁡ ( x 1 , x 2 , … ) f(x_{1},x_{2},\dots)  of bounded degree such that 
 

 
 
 f ⁡ ( x 1 , x 2 , … ) = f ⁡ ( x σ ⁡ ( 1 ) , x σ ⁡ ( 2 ) , … ) f(x_{1},x_{2},\dots)=f(x_{\sigma(1)},x_{\sigma(2)},\dots) 
 
 
 for every permutation  σ \sigma  of the positive integers fixing all but finitely many integers. See  Stanley [7, Chapter 7]  for an introduction to symmetric functions. A symmetric function is  homogeneous of degree  d d  if every monomial appearing in it with a nonzero coefficient has total degree  d d . The  elementary symmetric function  indexed by a partition  λ \lambda  is 
 

 
 
 e λ = e λ 1 ​ e λ 2 ​ ⋯ , e_{\lambda}=e_{\lambda_{1}}e_{\lambda_{2}}\dotsm, 
 
 
 where  e k e_{k}  is the sum of all products of  k k  distinct variables  x i x_{i} . Elementary symmetric functions form a basis of the algebra of symmetric functions. A symmetric function is  e e -positive  if all its  e e -coefficients are nonnegative. We will use the extended symmetric function  e I e_{I}  with composition index  I I , which is defined as 
 

 
 
 e I = e λ ⁡ ( I ) , e_{I}=e_{\lambda(I)}, 
 
 
 where  λ ⁡ ( I ) \lambda(I)  is the underlying partition whose parts are those of  I I . For a symmetric function  f f , any expansion 
 

 
 
 f = ∑ I ⊨ n c I ​ e I f=\sum_{I\vDash n}c_{I}e_{I} 
 
 
 in terms of  e I e_{I}  is called an  e I e_{I} -expansion  of  f f . An  e I e_{I} -expansion is said to be  positive  if  c I ≥ 0 c_{I}\geq 0  for all compositions  I I . Any symmetric function  f f  having a positive  e I e_{I} -expansion is  e e -positive. 
 
 
 Stanley [6, Definition 2.1]  introduced the chromatic symmetric function of a graph  G G , defining it as the formal power series 
 

 
 
 X G = ∑ κ x κ ⁡ ( v 1 ) ​ ⋯ ​ x κ ⁡ ( v n ) , X_{G}=\sum_{\kappa}x_{\kappa(v_{1})}\dotsm x_{\kappa(v_{n})}, 
 
 
 where  κ \kappa  runs over proper colorings of  G G , and  v 1 , … , v n v_{1},\dots,v_{n}  are the vertices of  G G . For instance, the complete graph  K n K_{n}  has chromatic symmetric function 
 

 
 
 X K n = n ! ​ e n . X_{K_{n}}=n!e_{n}. 
 
 
 
 
 We introduce the functions  w I ′ w_{I}^{\prime}  and  w I w_{I}  defined for compositions  I I  by 
 

 
 
 w I ′ = ( i 2 − 1 ) ​ ( i 3 − 1 ) ​ ⋯ and w I = i 1 ​ w I ′ . w_{I}^{\prime}=(i_{2}-1)(i_{3}-1)\dotsm\quad\text{and}\quad w_{I}=i_{1}w_{I}^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Let  P n P_{n}  denote the  n n -vertex path.  Shareshian and Wachs [5, Table 1]  obtained the following formula. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.1   ( Shareshian and Wachs ) . 
 
 X P n = ∑ I ⊨ n w I ​ e I X_{P_{n}}=\sum_{I\vDash n}w_{I}e_{I}  for any  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 . 
 
 
 
 For instance, 
 

 
 
 X P 3 = w 3 ​ e 3 + w 21 ​ e 21 + w 12 ​ e 12 + w 111 ​ e 111 = 3 ​ e 3 + e 12 . X_{P_{3}}=w_{3}e_{3}+w_{21}e_{21}+w_{12}e_{12}+w_{111}e_{111}=3e_{3}+e_{12}. 
 
 
 
 
 Zheng [14, Lemma 4.4]  presented the following formula for the chromatic symmetric functions of  3 3 -legged spiders. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.2   ( Zheng ) . 
 
 Let  n ≥ 4 n\geq 4 . For any partition  ( a , b , c ) ⊢ n − 1 (a,b,c)\vdash n-1 , 
 

 
 
 X S ⁡ ( a , b , c ) = X P n + ∑ i = 1 c ( X P i ​ X P n − i − X P b + i ​ X P n − b − i ) . X_{S(a,b,c)}=X_{P_{n}}+\sum_{i=1}^{c}(X_{P_{i}}X_{P_{n-i}}-X_{P_{b+i}}X_{P_{n-b-i}}). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 3.  Confirming the  e e -positivity of spiders  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1) 

 
 Throughout this section, we let  m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 ,  n = 6 ​ m + 4 n=6m+4 ,  G = S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) G=S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1) , and 
 

 
 
 𝒞 d = { I ⊨ d : i 1 , i 2 , ⋯ ≥ 2 } for any  d . \mathcal{C}_{d}=\{I\vDash d\colon i_{1},i_{2},\dots\geq 2\}\quad\text{for any $d$.} 
 
 
 Our goal is to find a positive  e I e_{I} -expansion of  X G X_{G} . First, we derive an  e I e_{I} -expansion for  X G X_{G} . 
 
 
 Lemma 3.1 . 
 
 For any  m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 , 
 

 
 
 X S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) \displaystyle X_{S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1)} 
 = e 1 2 ​ ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 2 , Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ) ≥ 1 w 1 ​ I ​ e I + e 1 ​ X 1 + X 0 , \displaystyle=e_{1}^{2}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-2},\ \Theta_{I}(4m+2)\geq 1}w_{1I}e_{I}+e_{1}X_{1}+X_{0}, 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 X 1 \displaystyle X_{1} 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 1 Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ) ≥ 2 w I ​ e I + ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 1 Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ) ≥ 1 w 1 ​ I ​ e I + ∑ J ∈ 𝒞 4 ​ m + 2 K ∈ 𝒞 2 ​ m + 1 ( k 1 ​ j 1 − 2 ​ j 1 + 1 ) ​ w J ′ ​ w K ′ ​ e J ​ K , and \displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-1}\\
\Theta_{I}(4m+2)\geq 2\end{subarray}}w_{I}e_{I}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-1}\\
\Theta_{I}(4m+2)\geq 1\end{subarray}}w_{1I}e_{I}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\in\mathcal{C}_{4m+2}\\
K\in\mathcal{C}_{2m+1}\end{subarray}}(k_{1}j_{1}-2j_{1}+1)w_{J}^{\prime}w_{K}^{\prime}e_{J\!K},\quad\text{and} 
 
 
 (3.1) 
 
 X 0 \displaystyle X_{0} 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n w I ​ e I − ∑ ( P , Q ) ∈ 𝒞 4 ​ m + 3 × 𝒞 2 ​ m + 1 w P ​ w Q ​ e P ​ Q . \displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n}}w_{I}e_{I}-\sum_{(P,Q)\in\mathcal{C}_{4m+3}\times\mathcal{C}_{2m+1}}w_{P}w_{Q}e_{PQ}. 
 
 
 As a consequence, the spider  S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1)  is  e e -positive if and only if  X 0 X_{0}  is  e e -positive. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  G = S ⁡ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m ,  1 ) G=S(4m+2,\,2m,\,1) . By  Lemmas   2.2  and  2.1 , 
 

 
 
 X G \displaystyle X_{G} 
 = X P n + X P 1 ​ X P n − 1 − X P 2 ​ m + 1 ​ X P 4 ​ m + 3 = ∑ I ⊨ n w I ​ e I + ∑ I ⊨ n − 1 w I ​ e 1 ​ I − ∑ J ⊨ 4 ​ m + 3 , K ⊨ 2 ​ m + 1 w J ​ w K ​ e J ​ K . \displaystyle=X_{P_{n}}+X_{P_{1}}X_{P_{n-1}}-X_{P_{2m+1}}X_{P_{4m+3}}=\sum_{I\vDash n}w_{I}e_{I}+\sum_{I\vDash n-1}w_{I}e_{1I}-\sum_{J\vDash 4m+3,\ K\vDash 2m+1}w_{J}w_{K}e_{J\!K}. 
 
 
 Arranging the terms according to the number of parts  1 1  in compositions, we obtain 
 

 
 
 X G = X 0 + e 1 ​ Y 1 + e 1 2 ​ Y 2 , X_{G}=X_{0}+e_{1}Y_{1}+e_{1}^{2}Y_{2}, 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 (3.2) 
 
 Y 1 \displaystyle Y_{1} 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 1 ( w 1 ​ I + w I ) ​ e I − ∑ J ∈ 𝒞 4 ​ m + 2 , K ∈ 𝒞 2 ​ m + 1 w 1 ​ J ​ w K ​ e J ​ K − ∑ J ∈ 𝒞 4 ​ m + 3 , K ∈ 𝒞 2 ​ m w J ​ w 1 ​ K ​ e J ​ K , and \displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-1}}(w_{1I}+w_{I})e_{I}-\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{4m+2},\,K\in\mathcal{C}_{2m+1}}w_{1J}w_{K}e_{J\!K}-\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{4m+3},\,K\in\mathcal{C}_{2m}}w_{J}w_{1K}e_{J\!K},\quad\text{and} 
 
 
 
 Y 2 \displaystyle Y_{2} 
 = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 2 w 1 ​ I ​ e I − ∑ J ∈ 𝒞 4 ​ m + 2 , K ∈ 𝒞 2 ​ m w 1 ​ J ​ w 1 ​ K ​ e J ​ K = ∑ I ∈ 𝒞 n − 2 , Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 2 ) ≥ 1 w 1 ​ I ​ e I . \displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-2}}w_{1I}e_{I}-\sum_{J\in\mathcal{C}_{4m+2},\,K\in\mathcal{C}_{2m}}w_{1J}w_{1K}e_{J\!K}=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{C}_{n-2},\,\Theta_{I}(4m+2)\geq 1}w_{1I}e_{I}.

 Now for any term  w P ​ w Q ​ e P ​ Q w_{P}w_{Q}e_{PQ}  in  X 0 X_{0} , it is natural to choose

 Proof.

 To find a positive  e I e_{I} -expansion for  Y Y , we give a positive  e I e_{I} -expansion for a part of  Y Y , and characterize the remaining part, say,  W W . We will give a positive  e I e_{I} -expansion for  W W  later. 
 
 
 Using the symmetry between  K K  and  L L  as compositions in the triple 
 

 
 
 ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 = 𝒞 2 ​ m + 2 × 𝒞 2 ​ m + 1 × 𝒞 2 ​ m + 1 , (J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}=\mathcal{C}_{2m+2}\times\mathcal{C}_{2m+1}\times\mathcal{C}_{2m+1}, 
 
 
 we use the lexicographic ordering for compositions: we write  K > L K>L  if there exists 
 

 
 
 1 ≤ s ≤ min ⁡ ( ℓ ⁡ ( K ) , ℓ ⁡ ( L ) ) 1\leq s\leq\min\mathopen{\big(}{\ell(K),\ell(L)}\mathclose{\big)}

 (3.16) 
 
 f ⁡ ( j , k , l ) = 2 ​ j ​ k ​ l − 3 ​ j ​ k − 3 ​ j ​ l − 2 ​ k ​ l + 2 ​ j + 2 ​ k + 2 ​ l . f(j,k,l)=2jkl-3jk-3jl-2kl+2j+2k+2l.

f(j,k,l)/2,&if $K=L$.\end{cases}

 Lemma 3.3 . 
 
 The symmetric function  Y Y  can be recast as 
 

 
 (3.17) 
 
 Y = ∑ ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 , K ≥ L , g ⁡ ( j 1 , k 1 , l 1 ) ≥ 0 g ⁡ ( j 1 , k 1 , l 1 ) ​ w J ′ ​ w K ′ ​ w L ′ ​ e J ​ K ​ L + W , Y=\sum_{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T},\ K\geq L,\ g(j_{1},\,k_{1},\,l_{1})\geq 0}g(j_{1},\,k_{1},\,l_{1})w_{J}^{\prime}w_{K}^{\prime}w_{L}^{\prime}e_{J\!K\!L}+W,

 𝐓 1 \displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{1}

 𝐓 2 \displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{2} 
 = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : l 1 = 2 , f ( j 1 , k 1 , 2 ) < 0 , k 1  is even,  k 1 ≥ 4 } , \displaystyle=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon l_{1}=2,\,f(j_{1},k_{1},2)<0,\,\text{$k_{1}$ is even, $k_{1}\geq 4$}\},

 𝐓 4 \displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{4} 
 = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : l 1 = 2 , k 1 = 2 , K ≥ L } . \displaystyle=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon l_{1}=2,\,k_{1}=2,\,K\geq L\}. 
 
 
 As a consequence,  Y Y  is  e e -positive if  W W  is  e e -positive. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We start from the expression for  Y Y  in  Eq.   3.6  by simplifying 
 

 
 
 ∑ ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 b ⁡ ( J , K , L ) ​ e J ​ K ​ L \sum_{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}}b(J,K,L)e_{J\!K\!L} 
 
 
 for  K = L K=L  and  K ≠ L K\neq L , respectively. Note that 
 

 
 
 ∑ ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 , K ≠ L b ⁡ ( J , K , L ) ​ e J ​ K ​ L = ∑ ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 , K > L ( b ⁡ ( J , K , L ) + b ⁡ ( J , L , K ) ) ​ e J ​ K ​ L , \sum_{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T},\,K\neq L}b(J,K,L)e_{J\!K\!L}=\sum_{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T},\,K>L}\mathopen{\big(}{b(J,K,L)+b(J,L,K)}\mathclose{\big)}e_{J\!K\!L}, 
 
 
 in which 
 

 
 
 b ⁡ ( J , K , L ) + b ⁡ ( J , L , K ) = f ⁡ ( j 1 , k 1 , l 1 ) ​ w J ′ ​ w K ′ ​ w L ′ , b(J,K,L)+b(J,L,K)=f(j_{1},\,k_{1},\,l_{1})w_{J}^{\prime}w_{K}^{\prime}w_{L}^{\prime}, 
 
 
 where  f f  is defined by  Eq.   3.16 . Then the terms for  K = L K=L  can be recast as 
 

 
 
 ∑ ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 , K = L b ⁡ ( J , K , L ) ​ e J ​ K ​ L = 1 2 ​ ∑ ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 , K = L f ⁡ ( j 1 , k 1 , l 1 ) ​ w J ′ ​ w K ′ ​ w L ′ ​ e J ​ K ​ L . \sum_{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T},\,K=L}b(J,K,L)e_{J\!K\!L}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T},\,K=L}f(j_{1},\,k_{1},\,l_{1})w_{J}^{\prime}w_{K}^{\prime}w_{L}^{\prime}e_{J\!K\!L}. 
 
 
 It follows that 
 

 
 
 Y = ∑ ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 , K ≥ L g ⁡ ( j 1 , k 1 , l 1 ) ​ w J ′ ​ w K ′ ​ w L ′ ​ e J ​ K ​ L + ∑ I ∈ 𝒜 w I ​ e I . Y=\sum_{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T},\,K\geq L}g(j_{1},\,k_{1},\,l_{1})w_{J}^{\prime}w_{K}^{\prime}w_{L}^{\prime}e_{J\!K\!L}+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{A}}w_{I}e_{I}. 
 
 
 Comparing it with the desired  Eq.   3.17 , it suffices to show 
 

 
 (3.18) 
 
 𝐓 ′ = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : K ≥ L , f ( j 1 , k 1 , l 1 ) < 0 } . \mathbf{T}^{\prime}=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon K\geq L,\,f(j_{1},\,k_{1},\,l_{1})<0\}. 
 
 
 
 
 We claim that 
 

 
 
 f ⁡ ( j , k , l ) ≥ 0 unless  ( j , k , l ) = ( 2 , 3 , 3 )  or  min ⁡ { k , l } = 2 . f(j,k,l)\geq 0\quad\text{unless $(j,k,l)=(2,3,3)$ or $\min\{k,\,l\}=2$}. 
 
 
 In fact, it is routine to verify that 
 

 
 
 f ⁡ ( j , k , l ) = 0 for  ( j , k , l ) ∈ { ( 3 , 3 , 3 ) , ( 2 , 3 , 4 ) , ( 2 , 4 , 3 ) } . f(j,k,l)=0\quad\text{for $(j,k,l)\in\{(3,3,3),\,(2,3,4),\,(2,4,3)\}$}. 
 
 
 We observe that the function  f f  is increasing in  k k , since 
 

 
 
 ∂ f ⁡ ( j , k , l ) ∂ k = ( j − 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ l − 3 ) − 1 ≥ 0 . \frac{\partial f(j,k,l)}{\partial k}=(j-1)(2l-3)-1\geq 0. 
 
 
 It is then increasing in  l l  by symmetry. On the other hand, since 
 

 
 
 ∂ f ⁡ ( j , k , l ) ∂ j = ( 2 ​ k − 3 ) ​ ( 2 ​ l − 3 ) − 5 2 , \frac{\partial f(j,k,l)}{\partial j}=\frac{(2k-3)(2l-3)-5}{2}, 
 
 
 the function  f f  is increasing in  j j  when  k + l ≥ 6 k+l\geq 6 . This proves the claim, and ( 3.18 ) follows.
∎ 
 
 
 
 It remains to find a positive  e I e_{I} -expansion for  W W . We will find injections
 φ i : 𝐓 i → 𝒜 \varphi_{i}\colon\mathbf{T}_{i}\to\mathcal{A}  for  1 ≤ i ≤ 3 1\leq i\leq 3  such that

 is  e e -positive and the sets
 φ 1 ​ ( 𝐓 1 ) \varphi_{1}(\mathbf{T}_{1}) ,
 φ 2 ​ ( 𝐓 2 ) \varphi_{2}(\mathbf{T}_{2})  and
 φ 3 ​ ( 𝐓 3 ) \varphi_{3}(\mathbf{T}_{3}) 
are pairwise disjoint, then show that 
 

 
 
 ∑ I ∈ 𝒜 \ φ 1 ​ ( 𝐓 1 ) \ φ 2 ​ ( 𝐓 2 ) \ φ 3 ​ ( 𝐓 3 ) w I ​ e I + ∑ ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 4 g ⁡ ( j 1 , k 1 , l 1 ) ​ w J ′ ​ w K ′ ​ w L ′ ​ e J ​ K ​ L \sum_{I\in\mathcal{A}\backslash\varphi_{1}(\mathbf{T}_{1})\backslash\varphi_{2}(\mathbf{T}_{2})\backslash\varphi_{3}(\mathbf{T}_{3})}w_{I}e_{I}+\sum_{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}_{4}}g(j_{1},\,k_{1},\,l_{1})w_{J}^{\prime}w_{K}^{\prime}w_{L}^{\prime}e_{J\!K\!L} 
 
 
 is  e e -positive. 
 
 
 As will be seen, for each triple  ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 i (J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}_{i} , 
 
 (1)  
 
 e J ​ K ​ L = e φ i ​ ( J , K , L ) e_{J\!K\!L}=e_{\varphi_{i}(J,K,L)} , 
 
 (2)  
 
 c i ​ ( J , K , L ) ≥ 0 c_{i}(J,K,L)\geq 0 , where 
 

 
 (3.19) 
 
 c i ​ ( J , K , L ) = w φ i ​ ( J , K , L ) w J ′ ​ w K ′ ​ w L ′ + f ⁡ ( j 1 , k 1 , l 1 ) . c_{i}(J,K,L)=\frac{w_{\varphi_{i}(J,K,L)}}{w_{J}^{\prime}w_{K}^{\prime}w_{L}^{\prime}}+f(j_{1},k_{1},l_{1}). 
 
 
 
 
 Since  f ⁡ ( j , k , l ) ≤ g ⁡ ( j , k , l ) < 0 f(j,k,l)\leq g(j,k,l)<0 , the condition  Item   2  above would guarantee the  e e -positivity of the following part in  W W : 
 

 
 (3.20) 
 
 w φ i ​ ( J , K , L ) ​ e φ i ​ ( J , K , L ) + g ⁡ ( j 1 , k 1 , l 1 ) ​ w J ′ ​ w K ′ ​ w L ′ ​ e J ​ K ​ L . w_{\varphi_{i}(J,K,L)}e_{\varphi_{i}(J,K,L)}+g(j_{1},k_{1},l_{1})w_{J}^{\prime}w_{K}^{\prime}w_{L}^{\prime}e_{J\!K\!L}. 
 
 
 
 
 Explicitly, we will verify the conditions  Items   1  and  2  above, and give a superset  𝒮 i ⊇ φ i ​ ( 𝐓 i ) \mathcal{S}_{i}\supseteq\varphi_{i}(\mathbf{T}_{i})  , see  Lemmas   3.4 ,  3.5  and  3.6 . For the case  i = 4 i=4 , we construct the map  φ 4 \varphi_{4}  in two pieces and provide supersets  𝒮 41 \mathcal{S}_{41}  and  𝒮 42 \mathcal{S}_{42}  for the corresponding images; see  Lemma   3.7 . Finally, we shall show in  Theorem   3.8  that these supersets are pairwise disjoint, which completes our proof for  Conjecture   1.1 . 
 
 
 Let us start by defining  φ 1 \varphi_{1} . For any

 Next, we shall show that  φ 1 \varphi_{1}  is injective. Suppose that 
 

 
 
 ( J ′ , K ′ , L ′ ) ∈ 𝐓 1 and P ​ K ​ R = φ 1 ​ ( J , K , L ) = φ 1 ​ ( J ′ , K ′ , L ′ ) = P ′ ​ K ′ ​ R ′ . (J^{\prime},K^{\prime},L^{\prime})\in\mathbf{T}_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad P\!K\!R=\varphi_{1}(J,K,L)=\varphi_{1}(J^{\prime},K^{\prime},L^{\prime})=P^{\prime}\!K^{\prime}\!R^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Let  I = P ​ K ​ R I=P\!K\!R . Since each of  P P  and  P ′ P^{\prime}  is the prefix of  I I  of size  2 ​ m + 3 2m+3 , we deduce that 
 

 
 
 P = P ′ . P=P^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Similarly, we deduce that 
 

 
 
 K = K ′ and R = R ′ . K=K^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad R=R^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Since  L L  (resp.,  L ′ L^{\prime} ) can be obtained from  R R  (resp.,  R ′ R^{\prime} ) by replacing the first part with  3 3 , we find 
 

 
 
 L = L ′ . L=L^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Since  P = P ′ P=P^{\prime} , we find 
 

 
 
 ℓ ⁡ ( J ) = ℓ ⁡ ( J ′ ) , and j s = j s ′ ​ for  2 ≤ s ≤ ℓ ⁡ ( J ) − 1 ; \ell(J)=\ell(J^{\prime}),\quad\text{and}\quad j_{s}=j_{s}^{\prime}\ \text{for $2\leq s\leq\ell(J)-1$}; 
 
 
 moreover, 
 

 
 
 min ⁡ { j − 1 ,  3 } = min ⁡ { j − 1 ′ ,  3 } and max ⁡ { j − 1 ,  3 } = max ⁡ { j − 1 ′ ,  3 } . \min\{j_{-1},\,3\}=\min\{j^{\prime}_{-1},\,3\}\quad\text{and}\quad\max\{j_{-1},\,3\}=\max\{j^{\prime}_{-1},\,3\}. 
 
 
 It follows that 
 

 
 
 j − 1 = j − 1 ′ . j_{-1}=j_{-1}^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Since  j 1 = 2 = j 1 ′ j_{1}=2=j_{1}^{\prime} , we find 
 

 
 
 J = J ′ . J=J^{\prime}. 
 
 
 This proves the injectivity of  φ 1 \varphi_{1}  and completes the proof.

 I ∈ 𝒜 ∩ 𝒮 2 . I\in\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{S}_{2}. 
 
 
 
 
 Next, we will show that  φ 2 \varphi_{2}  is injective. Suppose that 
 

 
 
 ( J ′ , K ′ , L ′ ) ∈ 𝐓 2 and J ​ Q ​ R = φ 2 ​ ( J , K , L ) = φ 2 ​ ( J ′ , K ′ , L ′ ) = J ′ ​ Q ′ ​ R ′ . (J^{\prime},K^{\prime},L^{\prime})\in\mathbf{T}_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad JQR=\varphi_{2}(J,K,L)=\varphi_{2}(J^{\prime},K^{\prime},L^{\prime})=J^{\prime}Q^{\prime}R^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Let  I = J ​ Q ​ R I=JQR . Since  I ∈ 𝒮 2 I\in\mathcal{S}_{2} , the composition  J J  is the prefix of  I I  of size  2 ​ m + 2 2m+2 , and so is  J ′ J^{\prime} . Thus 
 

 
 
 J = J ′ . J=J^{\prime}. 
 
 
 For the same reason, we have 
 

 
 
 Q = Q ′ and R = R ′ . Q=Q^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad R=R^{\prime}. 
 
 
 By definition, the composition  L L  (resp.,  L ′ L^{\prime} ) can be obtained by inserting a part  2 2 
at the beginning of  R R  (resp.,  R ′ R^{\prime} ). Since  R = R ′ R=R^{\prime} , we find 
 

 
 
 L = L ′ . L=L^{\prime}. 
 
 
 On the other hand, the composition  K K  (resp.,  K ′ K^{\prime} ) can be obtained from  Q Q  (resp.,  Q ′ Q^{\prime} ) by removing the first part and then moving the last part to the beginning. Since  Q = Q ′ Q=Q^{\prime} , we derive that 
 

 
 
 K = K ′ . K=K^{\prime}. 
 
 
 This proves the injectivity of  φ 2 \varphi_{2}  and completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Thirdly, let us define  φ 3 \varphi_{3} . For any triple 
 

 
 
 ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 3 = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : l 1 = 2 , f ( j 1 , k 1 , 2 ) < 0 , k 1  is odd } , (J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}_{3}=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon l_{1}=2,\,f(j_{1},k_{1},2)<0,\,\text{$k_{1}$ is odd}\}, 
 
 
 each of  K K  and  L L  contains at least one odd part since  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | K \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | L \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 2 ​ m + 1 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{K}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{L}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=2m+1 . We call a composition  even  if it has only even parts. For any composition  I I , if  I I  has at least one odd part, let  fo ⁡ ( I ) \operatorname{fo}(I)  (resp.,  lo ⁡ ( I ) \operatorname{lo}(I) ) be the index of the first (resp., last) odd part of  I I , and we use  i fo i_{\operatorname{fo}}  (resp.,  i lo i_{\operatorname{lo}} ) as a shorthand for  i fo ⁡ ( I ) i_{\operatorname{fo}(I)}  (resp.,  i lo ⁡ ( I ) i_{\operatorname{lo}(I)} ), which is the first (resp., last) odd part of  I I . If  I I  has no odd parts, then the expressions  fo ⁡ ( I ) − 1 \operatorname{fo}(I)-1  and  ℓ ⁡ ( I ) − lo ⁡ ( I ) \ell(I)-\operatorname{lo}(I) , whenever they occur, are both understood to be  ℓ ⁡ ( I ) \ell(I) ; in particular, they are  0 0  for  I = ∅ I=\emptyset . Define  φ 3 : 𝐓 3 → 𝒜 \varphi_{3}\colon\mathbf{T}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}  by 
 

 
 (3.24)

 in which the composition triple  ( P , Q , R ) (P,Q,R)  is exactly  ( J , Q , R ) (J,Q,R)  that is defined in  Eq.   3.24 . 
 
 (2)  
 
 The map  φ 3 \varphi_{3}  is injective. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  I = φ 3 ​ ( J , K , L ) = J ​ Q ​ R I=\varphi_{3}(J,K,L)=JQR . Let us check  I ∈ 𝒜 I\in\mathcal{A} . In fact, we have 
 

 
 
 Θ I ​ ( 2 ​ m + 1 ) = 1 , \Theta_{I}(2m+1)=1, 
 
 
 since  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | J \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 2 ​ m + 2 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{J}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=2m+2  and  j − 1 ≥ 2 j_{-1}\geq 2  by definition. On the other hand, assume to the contrary that  Θ I ​ ( 4 ​ m + 3 ) = 0 \Theta_{I}(4m+3)=0 . Since 
 

 
 
 \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | J \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | + \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 4 ​ m + 3 − k 1 , \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{J}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}+\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=4m+3-k_{1}, 
 
 
 the composition  R R  has a prefix of size  k 1 k_{1} .
By the definition of  R R , the prefix ending at the inserted part  k 1 k_{1}  is 
 

 
 
 l 1 ​ ⋯ ​ l s ​ k 1 , where  s = min ⁡ { fo ⁡ ( L ) − 1 , ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − lo ⁡ ( K ) + 1 } . l_{1}\dotsm l_{s}k_{1},\quad\text{where $s=\min\{\operatorname{fo}(L)-1,\,\ell(K)-\operatorname{lo}(K)+1\}$}. 
 
 
 Moreover, the parts  l 1 , … , l s l_{1},\dots,l_{s}  are all even. Hence a prefix of
 R R  can have size  k 1 k_{1}  only if  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | l 1 ​ ⋯ ​ l s \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 0 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{l_{1}\dotsm l_{s}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=0 , namely
only if  s = 0 s=0 . This is impossible, since 
 

 
 
 fo ⁡ ( L ) − 1 ≥ 1 and ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − lo ⁡ ( K ) + 1 ≥ 1 . \operatorname{fo}(L)-1\geq 1\quad\text{and}\quad\ell(K)-\operatorname{lo}(K)+1\geq 1. 
 
 
 This contradiction proves  I ∈ 𝒜 I\in\mathcal{A} . 
 
 
 Next, we will show that  I ∈ 𝒮 3 I\in\mathcal{S}_{3} . Since  l 1 = 2 l_{1}=2 , we find 
 

 
 
 fo ⁡ ( L ) − 1 ≥ 1 and r 1 = l 1 = 2 . \operatorname{fo}(L)-1\geq 1\quad\text{and}\quad r_{1}=l_{1}=2.

By definition, we see that 
 

 
 
 ℓ ⁡ ( Q ) − lo ⁡ ( Q ) = ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − lo ⁡ ( K ) . \ell(Q)-\operatorname{lo}(Q)=\ell(K)-\operatorname{lo}(K).

 If  fo ⁡ ( L ) − 1 ≤ ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − lo ⁡ ( K ) \operatorname{fo}(L)-1\leq\ell(K)-\operatorname{lo}(K) , then  Eq.   3.25  reduces to

 It follows that

 which is defined to be odd.

 It follows that 
 

 
 
 fo ⁡ ( R ) − 1 = ℓ ⁡ ( K ) − lo ⁡ ( K ) + 1 = ℓ ⁡ ( Q ) − lo ⁡ ( Q ) + 1 . \operatorname{fo}(R)-1=\ell(K)-\operatorname{lo}(K)+1=\ell(Q)-\operatorname{lo}(Q)+1. 
 
 
 
 
 This proves  I ∈ 𝒮 3 I\in\mathcal{S}_{3} . 
 
 
 Second, let us show that  φ 3 \varphi_{3}  is injective. Suppose that  ( J ′ , K ′ , L ′ ) ∈ 𝐓 3 (J^{\prime},K^{\prime},L^{\prime})\in\mathbf{T}_{3}  and 
 

 
 
 J ​ Q ​ R = φ 3 ​ ( J , K , L ) = φ 3 ​ ( J ′ , K ′ , L ′ ) = J ′ ​ Q ′ ​ R ′ , JQR=\varphi_{3}(J,K,L)=\varphi_{3}(J^{\prime},K^{\prime},L^{\prime})=J^{\prime}Q^{\prime}R^{\prime}, 
 
 
 where  Q Q ,  R R ,  Q ′ Q^{\prime}  and  R ′ R^{\prime}  are defined by  Eq.   3.24 . Let  I = J ​ Q ​ R I=JQR . By definition of  φ 3 \varphi_{3} , the composition  J J  is the prefix of  I I  of size  2 ​ m + 2 2m+2 , and so is  J ′ J^{\prime} . Thus 
 

 
 
 J = J ′ and Q ​ R = Q ′ ​ R ′ . J=J^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad QR=Q^{\prime}R^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Assume that  Q ≠ Q ′ Q\neq Q^{\prime} . Then  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | ≠ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}\neq\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}} . Suppose that  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | < \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}<\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}  without loss of generality. Then there exists a composition  M ≠ ∅ M\neq\emptyset  such that 
 

 
 
 Q ′ = Q ​ M and R = M ​ R ′ . Q^{\prime}=QM\quad\text{and}\quad R=MR^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Since  I ∈ 𝒮 3 I\in\mathcal{S}_{3} , 
 

 
 
 2 ​ m + 1 − r fo ′ = \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | > \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 2 ​ m + 1 − r fo . 2m+1-r^{\prime}_{\operatorname{fo}}=\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}>\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=2m+1-r_{\operatorname{fo}}. 
 
 
 It follows that 
 

 
 
 r fo > r fo ′ . r_{\operatorname{fo}}>r_{\operatorname{fo}}^{\prime}. 
 
 
 If  M M  had no odd part, then the first odd part of  R = M ​ R ′ R=MR^{\prime}  would be the first odd part of  R ′ R^{\prime} , of value  r fo ′ r_{\operatorname{fo}}^{\prime} , a contradiction. Thus  M M  has an odd part, and since  M M  is a prefix of  R R , the first odd part of  M M  is the first odd part of  R R , of value  r fo r_{\operatorname{fo}} . This proves 
 

 
 
 \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | ≥ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | + r fo = 2 ​ m + 1 , \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}\geq\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}+r_{\operatorname{fo}}=2m+1, 
 
 
 a contradiction. Thus 
 

 
 
 Q = Q ′ . Q=Q^{\prime}. 
 
 
 As a result, we obtain 
 

 
 
 R = R ′ . R=R^{\prime}. 
 
 
 In summary, we have 
 

 
 
 ( J , Q , R ) = ( J ′ , Q ′ , R ′ ) . (J,Q,R)=(J^{\prime},Q^{\prime},R^{\prime}). 
 
 
 Since  K K  (resp.,  K ′ K^{\prime} ) is obtained by inserting the first odd part of  R R  (resp.,  R ′ R^{\prime} ) at the beginning of  Q Q  (resp.,  Q ′ Q^{\prime} ), we find 
 

 
 
 K = K ′ . K=K^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Since  L L  (resp.,  L ′ L^{\prime} ) is obtained from  R R  (resp.,  R ′ R^{\prime} ) by removing the first odd part, we find 
 

 
 
 L = L ′ . L=L^{\prime}. 
 
 
 This proves the injectivity of  φ 3 \varphi_{3}  and completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Now let us handle  𝐓 4 \mathbf{T}_{4} . Recall that 
 

 
 
 𝐓 4 = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : k 1 = l 1 = 2 , K ≥ L } . \mathbf{T}_{4}=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon k_{1}=l_{1}=2,\,K\geq L\}. 
 
 
 We call a composition  odd  if it has only odd parts. For  ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 4 (J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}_{4} , let  U = U ⁡ ( K ) U=U(K)  be the composition obtained from  K K  by moving its longest odd suffix to the beginning; in other words, 
 

 
 
 U = K 2 ​ K 1 , U=K_{2}K_{1}, 
 
 
 where  K 2 K_{2}  is the longest odd suffix of  K K , and  K 1 K_{1}  is the prefix of  K K  such that  K = K 1 ​ K 2 K=K_{1}K_{2} . Then  u − 1 u_{-1}  is even, i.e., 
 

 
 (3.26) 
 
 ℓ ⁡ ( U ) − lo ⁡ ( U ) ≥ 1 . \ell(U)-\operatorname{lo}(U)\geq 1. 
 
 
 Note that  K 2 K_{2}  is empty if  k − 1 k_{-1}  is even; in that case  U = K U=K . For any  ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 4 (J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}_{4} , let  I = J ​ U ​ L I=JUL . 
 
 
 Define  φ 4 ​ ( J , K , L ) \varphi_{4}(J,K,L)  to be the composition obtained in the following  3 3  steps: 
 
 (1)  
 
 Insert a bar between the parts  u − 1 u_{-1}  and  l 1 l_{1}  in  I I . 
 
 (2)  
 
 Locate all odd parts of  I I  that are nearest to the bar. Since  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | K \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | L \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = 2 ​ m + 1 \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{K}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{L}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=2m+1 , such a part always exists. When such a part is not unique, the number of such parts must be two; in that case, we assign the part lying to the right of the bar to be the desired one. 
 
 (3)  
 
 Move this odd part to the symmetric position on the other side of the bar. 
 
 
 For example, 
 

 
 (3.27) 
 
 φ 41 ​ ( 86 , 2335 , 232222 ) \displaystyle\varphi_{41}(86,2335,232222) 
 = 863353222222 , and \displaystyle=863353222222,\quad\text{and} 
 
 
 (3.28) 
 
 φ 42 ​ ( 86 , 22333 , 222232 ) \displaystyle\varphi_{42}(86,22333,222232) 
 = 8633222232232 . \displaystyle=8633222232232. 
 
 
 Since  u − 1 u_{-1}  is even, we can infer that the odd part is not the one adjacent to the bar; thus 
 

 
 (3.29) 
 
 φ 4 ​ ( J , K , L ) ≠ J ​ U ​ L . \varphi_{4}(J,K,L)\neq JUL. 
 
 
 We will need  Eq.   3.29  in proving  φ 4 ​ ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝒜 \varphi_{4}(J,K,L)\in\mathcal{A} ; this is also the reason why we introduce the operation  U U . 
 
 
 For technical convenience, we give an algebraic definition for  φ 4 \varphi_{4} , which is equivalent to the combinatorial one above. Let 
 

 
 
 𝐓 4 ′ = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 , k 1 = l 1 = 2 } . \mathbf{T}_{4}^{\prime}=\{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T},\,k_{1}=l_{1}=2\}. 
 
 
 Then  𝐓 4 ⊂ 𝐓 4 ′ \mathbf{T}_{4}\subset\mathbf{T}_{4}^{\prime} , and  𝐓 4 ′ \mathbf{T}_{4}^{\prime}  decomposes as
 𝐓 4 ′ = 𝐓 41 ⊔ 𝐓 42 \mathbf{T}_{4}^{\prime}=\mathbf{T}_{41}\sqcup\mathbf{T}_{42} , where 
 

 
 
 𝐓 41 \displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{41} 
 = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : k 1 = l 1 = 2 , fo ( L ) − 1 ≤ ℓ ( U ( K ) ) − lo ( U ( K ) ) } , and \displaystyle=\mathopen{\big\{}{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon k_{1}=l_{1}=2,\,\operatorname{fo}(L)-1\leq\ell\mathopen{\big(}{U(K)}\mathclose{\big)}-\operatorname{lo}\mathopen{\big(}{U(K)}\mathclose{\big)}}\mathclose{\big\}},\quad\text{and} 
 
 
 
 𝐓 42 \displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{42} 
 = { ( J , K , L ) ∈ 𝐓 : k 1 = l 1 = 2 , fo ( L ) − 1 > ℓ ( U ( K ) ) − lo ( U ( K ) ) } . \displaystyle=\mathopen{\big\{}{(J,K,L)\in\mathbf{T}\colon k_{1}=l_{1}=2,\,\operatorname{fo}(L)-1>\ell\mathopen{\big(}{U(K)}\mathclose{\big)}-\operatorname{lo}\mathopen{\big(}{U(K)}\mathclose{\big)}}\mathclose{\big\}}.

$Q^{\prime}2M=Q$,
$2M\!R=R^{\prime}$, and
$\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{M}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=r_{\operatorname{fo}}^{\prime}$.} 
 
 
 Since  r fo ′ r_{\operatorname{fo}}^{\prime}  is odd,  M M  has at least one odd part. Since  R ′ = 2 ​ M ​ R R^{\prime}=2M\!R , the composition  M M  contains the first odd part of  R ′ R^{\prime} , which is  r fo ′ r_{\operatorname{fo}}^{\prime} . Thus  M M  consists of the single part  r fo ′ r^{\prime}_{\operatorname{fo}} . It follows that the last part of  Q = Q ′ ​ 2 ​ M Q=Q^{\prime}2M  is  r fo ′ r_{\operatorname{fo}}^{\prime} , which is odd. On the other hand, since  I ∈ 𝒮 2 I\in\mathcal{S}_{2} ,  q − 1 q_{-1}  is even, a contradiction. This proves 
 

 
 
 ( 𝒮 3 ∪ 𝒮 42 ) ∩ 𝒮 2 = ∅ . (\mathcal{S}_{3}\cup\mathcal{S}_{42})\cap\mathcal{S}_{2}=\emptyset. 
 
 
 
 (2)

Then we can suppose that  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | < \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}<\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}  without loss of generality. It follows that  r fo > r fo ′ r_{\operatorname{fo}}>r_{\operatorname{fo}}^{\prime} .  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | ≥ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | + r fo = 2 ​ m + 1 , \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}\geq\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}+r_{\operatorname{fo}}=2m+1, 
 
 
 a contradiction. This proves 
 

 
 
 \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | and r fo = r fo ′ . \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}\quad\text{and}\quad r_{\operatorname{fo}}=r_{\operatorname{fo}}^{\prime}. 
 
 
 Since  Q ​ R = Q ′ ​ R ′ QR=Q^{\prime}R^{\prime} , we deduce that  Q = Q ′ Q=Q^{\prime} . It follows that  R = R ′ R=R^{\prime} . In summary, we have 
 

 
 
 ( P , Q , R ) = ( P ′ , Q ′ , R ′ ) . (P,Q,R)=(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime},R^{\prime}). 
 
 
 Since  P ′ ​ Q ′ ​ R ′ ∈ 𝒮 42 P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{42} , we have 
 

 
 
 fo ⁡ ( R ′ ) − 1 ≤ ℓ ⁡ ( Q ′ ) − lo ⁡ ( Q ′ ) and fo ⁡ ( R ) − 1 ≤ ℓ ⁡ ( Q ) − lo ⁡ ( Q ) . \operatorname{fo}(R^{\prime})-1\leq\ell(Q^{\prime})-\operatorname{lo}(Q^{\prime})\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{fo}(R)-1\leq\ell(Q)-\operatorname{lo}(Q). 
 
 
 Thus the second alternative in the definition of  𝒮 3 \mathcal{S}_{3}  cannot occur.
Hence the first alternative must hold, and so  r fo ⁡ ( R ) + 1 r_{\operatorname{fo}(R)+1}  is odd.
This contradicts the condition  r fo ⁡ ( R ′ ) + 1 ′ r^{\prime}_{\operatorname{fo}(R^{\prime})+1}  is even in  𝒮 42 \mathcal{S}_{42} .
Therefore, 
 

 
 
 𝒮 3 ∩ 𝒮 42 = ∅ . \mathcal{S}_{3}\cap\mathcal{S}_{42}=\emptyset. 
 
 
 
 (4)  
 
 Assume that  ( 𝒮 3 ∪ 𝒮 42 ) ∩ 𝒮 41 ≠ ∅ (\mathcal{S}_{3}\cup\mathcal{S}_{42})\cap\mathcal{S}_{41}\neq\emptyset . Then there exists a composition 
 

 
 
 I = P ​ Q ​ R = P ′ ​ Q ′ ​ R ′ , I=PQR=P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}R^{\prime}, 
 
 
 where  P ​ Q ​ R ∈ 𝒮 3 ∪ 𝒮 42 PQR\in\mathcal{S}_{3}\cup\mathcal{S}_{42} ,  P ′ ​ Q ′ ​ R ′ ∈ 𝒮 41 P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{41} , 
 

 
 
 ( \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | P \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | , \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | , \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | R \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | ) \displaystyle(\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{P}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}},\,\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}},\,\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{R}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}) 
 = ( 2 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m + 1 − r fo ,  2 ​ m + 1 + r fo ) , and \displaystyle=(2m+2,\,2m+1-r_{\operatorname{fo}},\,2m+1+r_{\operatorname{fo}}),\quad\text{and} 
 
 
 
 ( \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | P ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | , \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | Q ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | , \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | R ′ \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | ) \displaystyle(\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{P^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}},\,\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{Q^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}},\,\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{R^{\prime}}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}) 
 = ( 2 ​ m + 2 ,  2 ​ m + 1 + q lo ′ ,  2 ​ m + 1 − q lo ′ ) . \displaystyle=(2m+2,\,2m+1+q^{\prime}_{\operatorname{lo}},\,2m+1-q^{\prime}_{\operatorname{lo}}). 
 
 
 As before, we have 
 

 
 
 P = P ′ , Q ​ R = Q ′ ​ R ′ , P=P^{\prime},\quad QR=Q^{\prime}R^{\prime}, 
 
 
 and we can write 
 

 
 
 I = P Q M R ′ , where  Q ​ M = Q ′ ,  M ​ R ′ = R , and  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | M \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = r fo + q lo ′ . I=PQM\!R^{\prime},\quad\text{where
$QM=Q^{\prime}$,
$M\!R^{\prime}=R$, and
$\mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{M}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=r_{\operatorname{fo}}+q^{\prime}_{\operatorname{lo}}$.} 
 
 
 We proceed according to the number of odd parts of  M M . 
 
 
 Suppose that  M M  has no odd parts. Since  Q ​ M = Q ′ QM=Q^{\prime} , we have 
 

 
 
 ℓ ⁡ ( Q ) − lo ⁡ ( Q ) + 1 ≤ ℓ ⁡ ( Q ′ ) − lo ⁡ ( Q ′ ) ; \ell(Q)-\operatorname{lo}(Q)+1\leq\ell(Q^{\prime})-\operatorname{lo}(Q^{\prime}); 
 
 
 since  R = M ​ R ′ R=M\!R^{\prime} , we have 
 

 
 
 fo ⁡ ( R ) − 1 ≥ fo ⁡ ( R ′ ) ; \operatorname{fo}(R)-1\geq\operatorname{fo}(R^{\prime}); 
 
 
 since  P ′ ​ Q ′ ​ R ′ ∈ 𝒮 41 P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{41} , we have 
 

 
 
 fo ⁡ ( R ′ ) − 1 ≥ ℓ ⁡ ( Q ′ ) − lo ⁡ ( Q ′ ) . \operatorname{fo}(R^{\prime})-1\geq\ell(Q^{\prime})-\operatorname{lo}(Q^{\prime}). 
 
 
 Therefore, 
 

 
 (3.32) 
 
 fo ⁡ ( R ) − 1 ≥ fo ⁡ ( R ′ ) ≥ ℓ ⁡ ( Q ′ ) − lo ⁡ ( Q ′ ) + 1 ≥ ℓ ⁡ ( Q ) − lo ⁡ ( Q ) + 2 , \operatorname{fo}(R)-1\geq\operatorname{fo}(R^{\prime})\geq\ell(Q^{\prime})-\operatorname{lo}(Q^{\prime})+1\geq\ell(Q)-\operatorname{lo}(Q)+2, 
 
 
 which is impossible since  P ​ Q ​ R ∈ 𝒮 3 ∪ 𝒮 42 PQR\in\mathcal{S}_{3}\cup\mathcal{S}_{42} . 
 
 
 Otherwise,  M M  has at least two odd parts, since  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | M \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = r fo + q lo ′ \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{M}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=r_{\operatorname{fo}}+q^{\prime}_{\operatorname{lo}}  is even. Thus the first and last odd parts of  M M  are distinct. Since  Q ′ = Q ​ M Q^{\prime}=QM , the last odd part of  M M  has value  q lo ′ q_{\operatorname{lo}}^{\prime} ; since  R = M ​ R ′ R=MR^{\prime} , the first odd part of  M M  has value  r fo r_{\operatorname{fo}} . Therefore, the composition  M M  contains two parts: one is of value  r fo r_{\operatorname{fo}} , the other is of value  q lo ′ q_{\operatorname{lo}}^{\prime} . Together with  \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | M \@mathmeasure \big@size 1 \big@size | = r fo + q lo ′ \mathopen{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}{M}\mathclose{\mathchoice{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 1\big@size\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.7\big@size\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}{{\@mathmeasure{}{\big@size 0.5\big@size\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.}}}}=r_{\operatorname{fo}}+q_{\operatorname{lo}}^{\prime} , we derive that  M = r fo ​ q lo ′ M=r_{\operatorname{fo}}\,q_{\operatorname{lo}}^{\prime} . Since  R = M ​ R ′ R=MR^{\prime} , we obtain  r 1 = r fo r_{1}=r_{\operatorname{fo}}  is odd. Since  P ​ Q ​ R ∈ 𝒮 3 ∪ 𝒮 42 PQR\in\mathcal{S}_{3}\cup\mathcal{S}_{42} , we have  r 1 = 2 r_{1}=2 , a contradiction. This proves 
 

 
 
 ( 𝒮 3 ∪ 𝒮 42 ) ∩ 𝒮 41 = ∅ . (\mathcal{S}_{3}\cup\mathcal{S}_{42})\cap\mathcal{S}_{41}=\emptyset. 
 
 
 
 
 In summary, the sets
 𝒮 2 \mathcal{S}_{2} ,
 𝒮 3 \mathcal{S}_{3} ,
 𝒮 41 \mathcal{S}_{41} 
and  𝒮 42 \mathcal{S}_{42}  are pairwise disjoint. Together with the injectivity of  φ 41 \varphi_{41}  and  φ 42 \varphi_{42} , the disjointness of  𝒮 41 \mathcal{S}_{41}  and  𝒮 42 \mathcal{S}_{42}  shows that the positive terms used for  𝐓 4 \mathbf{T}_{4}  are distinct, except for the intentional case  K = L K=L  handled above by using the common image only once. This completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 Acknowledgments 

 
 The authors are grateful to the anonymous referee for a careful reading of the manuscript and for helpful comments and suggestions. The referee’s comments helped us improve the presentation of the motivation in the introduction. This paper was completed while the second author was visiting Professor Jean-Yves Thibon at LIGM, Université Gustave Eiffel; he thanks Professor Thibon and LIGM for their hospitality.
